ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e4262	Mr. Federal Minister! My question is: [[/PAKT/VHG/XX/M/M_00152/index.shtml -- 152/M]] How does the work of the Tax Reform Commission work in the area of ecologizing the Austrian tax system?
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e4289	Please, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e4305	Ladies and gentlemen! With April 1997, I appointed the Tax Reform Commission under the direction of Professor Stoll. This tax reform commission has received a number of orders with the addition to tabling nothing at all, but in principle to review the question of the Austrian tax model also compared with other countries. I believe that the most important issues to be examined by the Tax Reform Commission are three areas. First of all, it is about the question of more favourable taxation of the production factor at work. I believe that it is undisputed that we should try to make the labour factor cheaper, but not in the form that wages are reduced, but in the form that the tax burden and the additional labour costs are minimized. It is then necessary to consider where the State receives its revenue. It is therefore legitimate to reconsider the system of capital taxation and, last but not least, resource taxation. A total of eight sub-groups were set up by the Tax Reform Commission, which deal with individual areas in a technical way, with the aim of considering certain aspects over a period of six to nine months. The Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection, No 4 concerns taxation of resources and eco-taxes. This technical committee is composed of a number of outstanding scientists, the social partners, representatives of interests and the ministries concerned, as well as representatives of the federal states and the municipalities. To date, the specialist committee has spoken 4 twice. The meetings have so far ended in such a way that the first discussion of the subject papers has been made and procedural questions of the technical committee have been resolved. In a second step, these evaluations will now be bundled by thematic priorities and will be subjected to a first analysis of steering effects, impact on the performance of the collection of data and to a first analysis. I therefore assume that the first considerations, which are then quite worthy of discussion outside the committee of experts, will not be expected before the spring of 1998.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e4396	I think the greening of the tax system is a very central issue for the next few years. Tax policy is not only a matter for providing revenue to the State, but also a steering instrument – no matter how you want to see it: social policy or environmental policy. I believe that the taxation of resources, which should lead to the most economical way of dealing with the finite resources, is a command of time. However, I would like to point out that we cannot take on an isolated pioneering role if we do not want to burden other economic areas in Austria against our main trading partners. Therefore, the greening of the tax system is also a pan-European issue, a question to be clarified at least within economic and monetary union. The question of ecology of the tax system has been discussed by Commissioner Monti's tax group for some time. It is not easy to implement, but I believe that we Austrians have very clear and certain positions in this regard. To that end, I hope to receive very valuable proposals from this working group of the Tax Reform Commission, which, after the political debate, we can see as a common concern of Austria, which we want to implement within the European Union. I know there are very offensive and very active partners in some countries.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e4733	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e4749	Ladies and gentlemen! I believe that the necessary measures to minimise costs in the public sector – because, of course, personnel costs are a very important factor – are taking all areas, of course also the financial sector. The Federal Government has set itself the goal of consolidating expenditure in the public service, and this also corresponds to the medium-term budget programme, which has been set to limit staff costs by the year 2000 by plus 1.3 percent per year. This requires action, because without measures, annual expenditure growth would amount to about 3 per cent by reference to a moderate salary increase. This objective can only be achieved if there are structural measures which can be represented by the staff and have a better distribution of work to the staff or a more decent remuneration. This means that the objective is to replace only a part of those staff who leave by retirement. This means, on the other hand, that not everything can work with work compression and that one cannot do it linearly and mathematically, because in certain areas the qualification plays a major role. The method of re-occupying only part of the posts after retirement is not possible in certain areas of the Federal Service. For example, think of the entire security area, where the principle that only part of the posts can be re-occupied after retirement does not apply. Of course – and this is also known to me – financial management is a very central part of public administration. Therefore, the measures used to change, facilitate and simplify working processes are of crucial importance. I refer, for example, to the Fit 2001 programme, which is why we are trying to develop ways of working together with the staff and their representatives. For who else than the people who are actually involved in the work, should know this better? We will have completed the technical IT equipment in the financial offices and in the financial services until autumn. In a few months, 5 000 such devices will be purchased to create the technical conditions for tax advisors, notaries and other professional groups in constant contact with the financial offices to carry out certain calculations, such as basic acquisition, inheritance tax and the like. I assume that those measures, which are being put in place, are appropriate to carry out the tasks of the financial management to date and, as I hope, to an optimal extent.

Point Report of the Federal Government's External Policy Committee on the 1996 Foreign Policy Report (III-89/833 of the Supplements) 2. Point Declaration by the Federal Minister for Foreign Affairs
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e6207	We come to points 1 and 2 of today's agenda. No oral reporting on item 1 has been made available. Anyway, we're going to do that. I can now give the floor to the Minister for Foreign Affairs to give his statement. - Please, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e6237	Thank you, Mr President. - High house! Foreign policy serves major objectives for a country, namely security and stability, economic well-being and, ultimately, peace. At present, Austrian foreign policy is in a phase of enormous changes and upheavals, because we have decided, through the two-thirds majority in the 1994 referendum, to pursue these important and fundamental objectives of Austrian foreign policy not only but also in association with the other EU countries. This means a tremendous change in national priorities and rankings for us, gives us stronger weight on the one hand, but on the other hand also forces us to coordinate our policies more closely with the EU partners on the other. This, of course, gives rise to a new priority within Austrian foreign policy. The first and most important objective is the formulation of an Austrian position in Europe, the implementation and enforcement of our interests at European level. The second objective is an active and good neighbourhood policy in the wider sense. I count not only the immediate neighbours, but also the Balkans or countries such as Poland, Romania, Bulgaria and the Baltic States in the wider sense. And thirdly, we do not disregard our multinational, multilateral commitment as a UN seat. We are the only EU country that has become and remains a UN seat. Of course, we also have traditional engagements in the field of minority policy, human rights issues et cetera. This is the framework in which we are moving in which we are making foreign policy. And today, two years after integration into the European Union, we can say with good conscience that we have taken advantage of this opportunity, we have come back to the heart of Europe from the edge of Europe. We're more important today than before. After all, we have over 110 embassies or permanent representations here in Austria, and we now have a worldwide network of over 80 embassies by opening five new embassies and 19 more general consulates, cultural institutions and the like. So we are very well represented worldwide, and vice versa is also home to the world in Austria. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] This new challenge, however, also corresponds quantitatively and qualitatively to an extremely dense visitation programme. I was surprised myself when I only figured out the last two years. We, the Minister of Foreign Affairs and the Secretary of State, have completed 400 visits at this time, half to Austria, half to abroad. The visits here in Austria and the visits abroad have kept quite a balance, which in turn have been observed half by Foreign Minister and State Secretary. I would particularly like to stress the role of the Secretary of State here. She is still on an appointment at the moment, but will also be taking part in the debate. It has visited 13 Latin American states in the last two years alone, partly countries in which no Austrian government member has been visiting for 15 years. You can see from this how important the division of work in the Ministry of Foreign Affairs has become between the Minister and the Secretary of State. I would like to express my express thanks to her for her work. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] Now there are some specific points that have been of particular importance in the Austrian foreign policy of recent months. The work at the Intergovernmental Conference is, of course, at the forefront. I was, as you know, in the last 15 months of the Austrian chief negotiator, and I believe that this agreement is ultimately a very good way forward. No member country – I say this quite openly, unprotectedly and without any false modesty or exaggeration – has been able to spend 100 percent of its concerns. This is also not possible if 15 countries together want something that, in part, has very different priorities and interests. However, the Austrian commitment to the Intergovernmental Conference as a whole has been taken into account. It was therefore undoubtedly a success for us. The issues that we have addressed – human rights, employment, environmental protection, animal welfare – have been taken into account and have ultimately been included in the new European Treaty of Amsterdam, although they were still very critical at the beginning of the conference by a number of Member States. And do not forget that the question of human rights in the European Treaty will already have a special meaning because this is the first time that sanctions have been laid down. The Community will be able to provide for a Member State which does not comply with the rules on human rights to be temporarily deprived of its membership rights. Sanctions can be imposed on him, and the European Court of Justice is given special jurisdiction here. This guarantees that the European Community is really that group in the world that takes these questions particularly important, which, I believe, can be an example for other groups of countries. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] We have fought to include non-discrimination provisions in the Treaty, whether they are discrimination against the elderly or disabled, whether they are discrimination based on race, gender, religion or whatever. We have included in the Treaty positive provisions that can resolve discrimination through positive measures of equality, in particular between men and women. An important issue for us Austrians, because our country is still an external border of the European Union, is the subject of internal and external security. For us, therefore, a particular focus has been on the issue of cooperation between justice and police in the fight against organised crime. And in this area, I believe that a very big step has been taken for the next few years. While maintaining the principle of unanimity for at least five years and eight years is a maximum, it is at least then a full community with control by the European Court of Justice, harmonisation of the case law and full operationality between the police and judicial authorities. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] This point seems to me to be very important because there is a balance and a link here. If we really want economic freedom to be spoken, freedom of establishment, freedom of establishment, freedom of establishment and the search for work within the European Union wherever you want, then this freedom of citizens must also face the cooperation of police and judicial authorities, otherwise we clearly have the wild freedom for organized criminals who can transfer billions of money from one country to another, from one country to another, at the touch of a button, and the police and the justice have been lagging behind for months. No one can want that, and that is why this point is crucial to the European Union's acceptance in the hearts and minds of its citizens. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] And I would add something that was, of course, discussed controversially here in Austria: that for us there is a clear link between the integration of the existing Schengen Treaty into the European Treaty and the current provisions of the implementation of the Schengen Treaty. It would be absurd if, for example, the Danes, the Irish or the British were to be laborious and very cautious, very hesitant to grant an exception on time, an opt-out or opt-in clause, and at the same time to another country that wants to join and can join, confirmed by an international Commission – where we have invested billions in expanding our airports, in logistics, in computers, in border control – if one keeps such a country artificially longer than necessary outside. That is why there is this context in terms of content, and I also wanted to make this clear and clear to the Austrian Parliament, but also to the European public. From the very beginning, Austria has insisted that, in particular, at the Intergovernmental Conference, we are dealing with those issues which are of great relevance to the citizen. This is mainly the employment issue. In Europe, there are millions of unemployed, millions of people who do not have a job. Austria is in the unemployment scale Thanks to God, it is relatively far below. However, it must be added that the value is high in view of our own history and experience of employment and unemployment. I am the last one who believes that, at European level, jobs can now be created directly, that would be an illusion. What we can do, however, is to coordinate at European level by sharing experiences between ourselves, by trying to share what we have learned with others the mistakes that one country has made, so that overall, a more coordinated employment policy is achieved. That is why I fully support the idea of the French, the new Prime Minister Jospin and President Chirac, in the autumn of this year, probably at the end of November, to hold a European employment summit of its own, on this issue: What can we do to create more jobs, more jobs for our young people, but also for older unemployed people? This is the first time we have been working on it. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] It was an Austrian initiative - I would like to say this very clearly - by Viktor Klima and I, that we are trying to prepare this summit in such a way that the social partners in Austria and in the other Member States are already involved in the preparation of the issues for this employment summit, but also at European level, the European social partners – and we have one of them, the President of the European Trade Union Confederation – can really participate in this employment initiative. Because the wider we support the initiative, the greater the success in implementing it will probably be. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] In the area of the common foreign and security policy, there was no major breakthrough, but there are important small steps to improve. The fact that the European Union finally has a planning cell that prepares foreign policy issues, which can lead to a more fundamental debate, I think is very important. The fact that there will not be five Commissioners, who will make each other competitive foreign policy in the future, is an advantage: It will only be a Commissioner. The fact that this one then participates in the Troika, a Secretary-General of the Council constantly deals with foreign policy, is a huge advantage and will ultimately help to improve the quality of European foreign policy. In addition, it has succeeded in achieving that in the future it will be harder to block European foreign policy decisions. This will help to ensure that there are majority voting where a European strategy has been unanimously adopted. This will help a country not to vote against or to vote against it, but rather to go a third way, namely that of abstaining from the construction; and this will help a country to be able to call on a national safeguard clause as an emergency brake, when vital interests of that country are affected. I also think it is positive that we have included the so-called Petersberg tasks, namely peacekeeping, enforcement, crisis management, humanitarian aid, in the Treaty. And by the way, it is very interesting that these Petersberg tasks 1: 1 correspond to the so-called peace support operations of NATO, which are included in the expanded partnership program. It is therefore very important, I believe, for Petersberg's tasks to be included in the Treaty within the European Union at both levels, in NATO - PfP-Plus, which has also enabled non-members, non-full members, to be better integrated into the planning and implementation process. I also say to the House that the true winner in the institutional struggle, if one wants to call it, is the European Parliament within the European Union. While Parliament has not been satisfied with all the wishes, – and we Austrians, together with other small countries, have done and fought very much – the parliamentarians have made significant improvements in the codecision at European level. There is a significant increase in the European Parliament's right of co-decision, a reduction in the number of procedures, a better involvement of national parliaments, a strengthening of the President of the Commission, an upgrading of the Committee of the Regions and new functions for the European Court of Justice. I think we can be quite satisfied with this new institutional balance. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] Ladies and gentlemen! We are very dissatisfied with the question of majority voting versus unanimity. We would have wished that we would be much more courageous than then, in the end, the way to majority voting. Austria has actually been one of the countries most radically demanding the extension of the rule of majority voting. We couldn't get through here. But I also say quite openly: As long as we are still negotiating many parts intergovernmental, that is national, there are, of course, also sensitive points for us, such as water, water use, energy use or soil law, in which we too insist on the rule of unanimity, and only when we come to a better vote can we also talk about these issues. This time the time was not ripe, and therefore we are not really satisfied with this question of balance between unanimity and multi-voice, but here one will have to drill just further. On the question of the composition of the Commission or the weighting of votes, we have kept our line. We were particularly concerned about not having to take any change to our loads. Here we wanted to maintain and safeguard the acquis in the national interest. That's a complete success. It was not a very easy fight, you can believe it, but it has become an Austrian success. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] A second important European issue is the preparation for the presidency, which we will take over in a year and which, of course, is a very important and first-time objective, a real challenge for ourselves. The President's task is not so much to put his own priorities, his own initiatives, his own projects in the foreground – you can do that too – but the most important thing is to put Europe's voice in a difficult time, really coordinate and try to develop and advance the European agenda. In this area, the Austrian Presidency will in particular require the final implementation of the euro decisions and the organisational and institutional fine-tuning. And believe me: I consider the introduction of the euro, a stable, strong European currency, to be the most likely project in the next few years and decades to secure the location of Europe, but also the location of Austria and thus its jobs. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] Under the Austrian Presidency, the final decisions for 1999 will be to be made, with the magic year 1999 not yet making the euro visible to the individual citizen, but it will make the project irreversible, because from 1999 onwards, the currencies participating in it will be inseparably restricted, the project will run, from this point on, Europe will be able to benefit from the location of a global central currency for itself, for its businesses, for its jobs, and from then on we will participate in this important integration project. The second very important project, which directly concerns us, is the enlargement of the European Union. You know that yesterday, in a full-day clause, the European Commission discussed the issue of the evaluation of the eleven candidates and next week, on 16 November. In July 1997, the comprehensive and comprehensive report will be presented to the public, to the ambassadors, to the European Parliament and thus also to the Member States. The interesting thing is that, based on an irrevocable ceiling in the financial framework of 1.27 percent of gross domestic product, which cannot be increased, we can ensure that, depending on the calculation, between 500 and 600 billion schilling will be available to the new Member States in the period 1999 to 2006. This is a lot of money, and I therefore believe that the Union is well equipped to deal with the inclusion of new Member States without taking advantage of additional taxpayers' money in the already existing Member States. I think this first statement by the Commission is very important because it also increases the acceptance of citizens in individual countries, to meet and welcome the reception of new members with open hearts. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] It is said that yesterday the Commission, in addition to a clear positive Avis, gave a positive response to five of our neighbouring countries plus Cyprus. The Commission therefore considers that negotiations with the Czech Republic, Poland, Hungary, Estonia and Slovenia could begin immediately as well as with Cyprus. I say quite openly, however, that we Austrians – and I hope that we have a consensus beyond the party’s borders – will not use us for a group model, but I still want to insist on the start line, the start of the negotiations with all the candidates, so that we do not divide into a first and second class of candidate countries. Of course, it will be so that the negotiations will automatically differ, but it would be wrong if we were to inform individual countries already at the beginning of the negotiations: Friends, you're not welcome! This would lead to an immeasurable democratization, perhaps even a destabilization in the political or economic sphere, or a discouragement in the pre-incitement of the necessary reform process. I would therefore like to continue in the coming months to promote the start-up model, the start of negotiations with everyone, and then to talk about differentiation in faster and slower accession negotiations. Ladies and gentlemen! So much on the issue of EU. – But a very important issue in these days is, of course, the question of security policy. The Madrid Summit ended yesterday, and this Madrid Summit has once again made it clear to us that in these days and weeks a new NATO has been created or has already emerged. In its joint statement at a meeting of the participating States at the Euroatlantic Partnership Council, Austria underlined that the decision of the Alliance to include new members is exemplary for the process of creating a new NATO, all the more so as this decision has preceded a Founding Act with Russia and a Charter with Ukraine. I think it is very important that the question of Russia and Ukraine be put out of dispute and then decided on the invitation to three new members. It is particularly important that NATO has explicitly agreed to keep its doors open to other members. The Federal Chancellor spoke in Madrid of a significant signal to all states and stressed that we understood this decision as an invitation to all European countries to further develop their cooperation with Allianz to the extent they wished. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] I think we have also set an important mark on this point in Austria. Following the Amsterdam European Council, the Madrid Summit has now shown that the new NATO is the key to European security architecture, because this new NATO is a comprehensive security concept. It does not just want to see the military risk, which may not be so great in the near future, but the true risks in terrorism, in migration, in the transfer of nuclear material, in organised crime, in unresolved minority issues and the overall aspects of cooperation between the economy, the environment and science make this comprehensive concept of security absolutely important and necessary. It is a new NATO, which is not directed against someone but, in cooperation with the UN, the European Union, the WEU and the OSCE, advocates a comprehensive European security system, a new NATO that has not upgraded, but, on the contrary, reduced, reduced, and, on the contrary, reduced budgets worldwide. Vienna's concrete negotiations for the disarmament of heavy weapons, but above all for the unilateral disarmament of nuclear weapons in the areas of the new members, and for a new NATO that does not create new, functional pan-European instruments and structures with Russia, Ukraine and certainly also with the Baltic States. President Clinton has really described very well what it is all about now. "To do for Europe's East, what we did for Europe's West: defensive freedom, strong democracy, temper old rivals, have integration, provide a stable climate in which prosperity can grow." – I don’t think it’s better to express what this new NATO stands for. Madrid has shown that the most important decisions in European security and defence policy will continue to fall within the scope of NATO full members, and it is important to know that NATO has defined enlargement in Madrid as a continuous process. Negotiations with the Czech Republic, Poland and Hungary will be concluded by the end of 1997. Its accession is planned for April 1999 on the occasion of the 50th anniversary of the founding of the Alliance in Washington. Allow an open word from the Austrian perspective to these three newly invited members. We know what in the history of these three countries the annual figures for Hungary, 1968 for the Czech Republic, 1981 – war law – mean for Poland. I would like to welcome at this point – and I hope in your name too – this invitation and this truly historic step for these three countries that are friends of ours. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] In 1999 the Heads of State or Government of NATO wanted to undergo a review of the enlargement process. In this context, the Madrid Summit has also explicitly referred to the candidate countries of Romania and Slovenia. They are already the leaders of a second round of enlargement, even after the prevailing opinion of those involved in the discussions, and it is expected that the preparations for this second round will start at the beginning of next year. At the same time, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the NATO States is required to review the enlargement process on a regular basis. We have responded. We have already started work on the option report in an inter-ministerial working group coordinated by the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Federal Chancellor's Office and the Ministry of State Defense, and we will speed up the work on this option report and, as I believe, we will make progress. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] It is also important for us that NATO invited us at this meeting in Madrid to upgrade our former liaison office to a full message from NATO. The Federal Chancellor and I have agreed that Austria will respond positively to this invitation in these days. [[New condolences with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] The topic of neighbourhood policy, good neighbourhood with the regions in the immediate vicinity and further away: Austria is the country that has eight neighbouring countries like Germany – only Germany and Serbia have a similar number of neighbouring countries – and it is therefore especially important for a small country to live in peace, friendship, unhappiness with its neighbours. If I can start with a heart attack for all of us, with South Tyrol, then it can be said that, when Austria and Italy made the United Nations declaration of dispute on South Tyrol, the then UN Secretary General spoke of a model for the settlement of other bilateral problems in Europe. We also know, however, that there have been some votes at that time, including in South Tyrol, which have been skeptical, whether by this statement of dispute the train has become more autonomous or more right for the South Tyroleans. [[Abg. Meisinger: Not for nothing!]] The opposite was the case. Today we have best relations with Italy like never before in our history. A few days ago, we had an extensive discussion with the head of state and SVP party Brugger, and we were able to convince ourselves of the fact that the South Tyroleans had just been able to obtain new rights for street maintenance, for the establishment and control of authorities, that they were now in place to get more or less education policy in their hands, and that the discussions for the establishment of a Free University in Bolzano are such that, in cooperation with the State University in Innsbruck, they can optimally cover a very important fundamental issue for the South Tyrol population. My talks with Foreign Minister Dini on the verge of the Madrid conference confirmed that the Italian Government also sees a model development in a bilateral relationship. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] However, security in Europe cannot be discussed without taking account of the situation in the Balkans. It doesn't really want to hear anything like that. It would be a dangerous path that Europe went here. [[Congratulations with the ÖVP and with representatives of the SPÖ.]] It is therefore necessary to implement the Dayton process, the peace process, which is contractually guaranteed and which means free elections, freedom of expression, institutional establishment, economic assistance. We must come down a little from the observation of development at the great political level, at the macro level, we must go down to the level of the communities, to the level of monitoring, what happens in the communities and villages, where there is also the real power, in which the real tolerance can develop and develop. Those who have seen the fortifications at the Gazan border that go far beyond the extent of the legendary fortifications and barriers at the Charly checkpoint in Berlin at that time know that much has to be done and can also be done in this area. It must bring the international community of states economically concrete, visible successes so that President Arafat can bring fruit to its people in the peace process. However, security cooperation between Palestine and Israel must also be resumed and intensified, because the legitimate need for security for the Israeli population must be respected by everyone, whether inside or outside. We have been able to accommodate the first EU Monitoring Centre for Racism and Xenophobia in Vienna. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and SPÖ.]] The OSCE’s headquarters has been in Vienna for years – it is an increasingly important seat and an ever-increasing organization. Not so much in the sense that military security can also be given, but important issues such as election observation, minority protection, concrete assistance will be implemented by the OSCE. I believe that the Austrians have helped by using, for example, Estonia and Bosnia-Herzegovina, by means of an above-average participation in the Croatia mission and by systematically establishing an expert card that the OSCE has the position it is taking today. I talked about the disarmament negotiations in Vienna. In addition, we are glad and proud that we can take a very big step forward towards a non-atomic Europe. De facto is still stored on Western European soil about 300, 400 nuclear pilot bombs. The United States' ground-based nuclear weapons – you know that – are no longer available in Europe at all. A particularly important issue is the intensification of the rules on the use of mines and explosions, and Austria is on the front line so that it is a total ban on anti-personnel mines. The draft Treaty, which is currently under way, has been drawn up by Austria and is already supported by more than 70 states, and we expect to be able to reach a truly comprehensive agreement in autumn. Ladies and gentlemen! I come to the conclusion and do not wish to conclude this accountable report without mentioning the really sacrificing activity of my house's staff, especially in the consular and humanitarian field, both domestically and abroad. With few staff and truly modest resources, under the difficult conditions of activity abroad, excellent and qualified work is done there. Thank you very much for that. Thank you very much.

My contribution will focus on this. I have the impression that today's debate is also the expression of a vacuum in the foreign policy debate, and that other aspects of the discussion must have the effect of overcoming this deficit in some way. In fact, we face challenges that will not be easy to deal with. We must note that the global political situation has been in a transitional phase since 1989. In this position we have to deal with a single remaining superpower. It is a democratic superpower, well-noticed, but we must recognize that this state has had very adverse effects on a number of international organisations. Negative effects are also noticeable in terms of how the relationship between Europe and the USA has developed. The question now arises of a new form of stability and balance on the international scale. It is not a stability which, like the old order, is based on the competition of different systems or which is based on a new kind of competition such as the conflict of cultures described by Samuel Huntington, but on a stability based on cooperation, on the conformity of human rights and on the conformity with democratic, market economy and social principles. However, such stability also requires forces that are capable of stabilizing the supremacy of the US, which sometimes has an unpleasant effect. [[Intrusion of the Abg. Young.]] Mr. Jung! The question is: Is the European Union able to contribute to a more civilised balance in the world? ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e14669	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen. First of all, I would like to deal with the nuclear safety agreement. We share the criticism that this is a very soft agreement and that it leaves many areas of interpretation open. But we also see – like my previous speaker, Mrs Tichy-Schreder – that this is a possibility. And what we see above all and what we base our hopes on – and this is also part of the five-party proposal that we decided two days ago – is the will to make real use of the possibilities given to us by the agreement with the border nuclear power stations. Above all, that seems very important to me. We have also discussed and mentioned this in the Committee on Foreign Affairs. This concerns, for example, the Dukovany nuclear power plant and the Temelin nuclear power plant under construction. Of course, if you explain this in this direction, if the obligation is there – and the decision has been taken, the request has been accepted in this way – it is also to do so that the Austrian Federal Government will also act in this direction, that we say: Well, this is, as it were, a first step for us, which shows the will of the five parties, let us take the agreement seriously on these points! In this sense, we agree with this agreement, as we have already agreed in committee. I do not want to list everything here again, but the most important of all are the frontier power stations, and we should use our possibilities, especially the two power plants. It is right and it is therefore also important that we have come to this five-party proposal and decision because, as we know, this principle paper is prepared in the European Commission and is always given great attention to Austria's position on this issue, because we have traditionally done a lot in the past. It is true that nuclear power plants are only concerned with the production of energy from nuclear power plants. There is still a lot to be done, for example, in the field of nuclear weapons. In this context, I would like to make a motion for a resolution which is very important to me personally. In Europe, more than half of the countries are de facto a nuclear-free zone, from Sweden and Finland to Switzerland to the former Soviet Union countries. However, all the former Eastern Bloc States are de facto nuclear-free today, because they have been disarmed by the various disarmament agreements in recent years and do not currently have nuclear weapons. There are also countries that have decided on their own initiative and have said: We will continue to do so, we want to continue to do so, and we have also called on the other countries to do the same. Ladies and gentlemen! This is similar to the nuclear power plants. This is about EU enlargement. In this case, it is about NATO enlargement. There is a danger that nuclear weapons will be stationed in these countries with the NATO enlargement of the three countries, which are now in the first round. It's possible. It's just a good will of NATO so far that she says: We're not going to have a nuclear weapons station there. But this renunciation has two difficulties: The one difficulty is that it is a voluntary abandonment of NATO's side, without any international binding, a relinquishing which NATO can withdraw at any time, if it considers it to be correct. I don't want to philosophise how the majority would then be and how that could look. I am quite certain, however, that those who are for a nuclear-free zone would then remain in the minority and would have the chance to do so. But there is also the second side, there are countries like Poland, which say: We don't want that! Ladies and gentlemen! It seems very important to us to ensure that, in the course of the NATO enlargement negotiations, the Austrian Federal Government does everything possible to guarantee this contractual security of the practically nuclear-free zones in Europe. Some countries in Europe and some Heads of State or Foreign Ministers in Europe are not at all averse to this. It also depends, as in the case of nuclear power plants, on who is the force of the initiative, who sets out the initiatives, who brings them in, and who is committed to this proposal. I think it would be very good for Austria – and it would be a contribution to an active foreign policy, which we discussed and assembled for a very long time before and where we criticised that it is not present – to say: Well, we are not only nuclear power plants free, we are also nuclear-free, we see it as our task that – at least as a first step – this part of Europe, which is de facto nuclear-free, remains as well! In addition, some Asian countries have already joined this example. There is a trend in this direction. I mean, we have to do everything we can to support this. Again: The statements of intent are not enough! That is why I make the following request: [[Quoted print version removed]] Now I would like to say a few words about the two amendments tabled by our group, which are included in this package that we are now discussing. I am pleased that my colleague Mr Langthaler's request is approved of the effective measures to limit whaling. I see this as a very positive sign that Austria can put – in a small but important area. Finally, I would like to draw attention to the ratification of the International Convention of ILO No. 169 peoples in independent countries who are born and live in tribes. We have already decided on this agreement here in Parliament. Unfortunately, no steps have been taken to this end. I am pleased that there has also been a five-party decision in this respect, which shows that we are taking this seriously now. I would just like to make a brief comment on what is particularly important to us. We do not have native peoples, but the Convention has a significant impact on our economic and development policy. There are a number of contact points which I do not even want to list here, but a question that has always been up-to-date in recent years was, for example, the imports of raw materials from countries such as Nigeria. There are a number of fields where this directly affects us, such as the field of food exports, where I believe that in future we should make a coherent policy between development policy and foreign policy in the handling of this ILO agreement. ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e14793	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to deal with the government proposals concerning a Europe Agreement establishing an association between the EU and the Mediterranean and Slovenia. Two Europe Agreements are to be ratified. Both agreements make clear the objective of the EU's European policy, namely to establish a permanent basis for relations with the Mediterranean third countries in the perspective of a cooperative partnership. The aim of this policy is to support these Mediterranean countries in their efforts to develop a zone of peace and stability. After Tunisia and Israel, Morocco is the third country, the third Mediterranean partner country to negotiate such an association agreement. The main focus is on free trade. Among other things, this agreement – and this seems particularly important to me – provides for the prohibition of anti-competitive practices, an aspect which cannot be assessed high enough in the interests of a more intensive development of free trade. However, it seems particularly important to me to be the agreed objective of creating a free trade area, which is to be achieved through regional integration. This EU cooperation policy in the Mediterranean is the opposite of the policy of opening up to Central and Central Eastern Europe. In this way, the agreement to be approved today to establish an association with Slovenia is another step. Well, ladies and gentlemen, soon a few thoughts about enlargement. In addition to creating an economic and monetary union, I believe that the European Union's eastern enlargement is the second dominant theme of European integration policy. The political and economic integration of this region into the EU's structures is the best way to create stable and just social conditions in these countries in order to support these countries in their efforts to build a democratic society, democratic structures. A few days ago, the President of the EU, the Dutch Prime Minister Kok, invited the representatives of the 12 candidate countries and, in this context, provided them with the prospect of examining the relevant arrangements in order to open the way for this process. We welcome this. It must be clear, however, that the stability policy task, which applies to the enlargement of the EU, of course also applies to the Mediterranean. We believe that both EU enlargement and the successful development of the Mediterranean region are part of a pan-European stability policy. Ladies and gentlemen! If we ask ourselves how quickly and intensively the enlargement of the EU is to take place, then it makes sense to use the way of Austria as a comparison. 38 years have passed from the Treaties of Rome in 1957 to our full membership of the EU. I do not want to propose this time horizon for enlargement of the EU. But let us consider how close our economic, our income situation was to that of the EC countries, and yet we had to bring a long process with many obstacles, with many sidesteps – I think of the association agreements in the early 1970s – to full membership of the EU. That was necessary! If we look back today, we must honestly admit: Those were necessary steps! This process of integration took place in many small steps. I therefore believe that we do nothing good to the representatives of these countries and the citizens of these countries if we make them illusions and predict that EU accession can be dealt with quickly. I know many countries in the East very well. The differences in income between these countries and the countries of the EU are in a ratio of one to one to fifteen, not to mention the social differences. We must not, therefore, make the mistake of creating illusions in these countries which are not feasible. Ladies and gentlemen! I would define this for myself as the following: Easter enlargement as soon as possible, but as slowly as necessary. [[Congratulation of the Abg. Haigermoser.]] A realistic Easter enlargement policy must promote the creation of a framework that allows for a gradual, controlled integration process. It would make little sense and – I repeat – it would be really unfair for these countries to promise a rapid integration process. The corresponding hopes would very soon turn into great disappointments. [[Congratulation of the Abg. Haigermoser.]] Ladies and gentlemen! Finally, I would like to point out why it is so important, Mr Foreign Minister, to clarify our position in these crucial negotiations. The history of the last six seven years has shown that Austria was a profiter of the eastern opening, but it was also associated with many problems. I therefore believe that it is right to call on our negotiators to take realistic intermediate steps to overcome the existing obstacles and new ones. Wherever we can, be it for the transport infrastructure, for the support of joint ventures, for EU border crossings or for the development of contacts, we should help. But we must keep in mind that, in the light of the experience of recent years, we have been making this process for our country in such a way that it is implemented as slowly as I have said. - Thank you.

A committee of inquiry shall be set up for the purpose of Parliament's own parliamentary control. That is to say, in general, this is done by the Court of Auditors and in the very special case by Parliament itself. In other words, the Court of Auditors' control possibilities have of course to be automatically transferred to this subcommittee. What does § 99 para. 2? – This Section 99 para. 2 is given in Chapter 15: The Court of Auditors' contracts of inspection. That is to say, everyone knows the relevant principles for the interpretation and interpretation of laws, that the audit rules of the Court of Auditors must be applied accordingly. There's inside: "An examination of the conduct must also be carried out without the decision of the National Council" and "to refer to a certain process in a matter of federal conduct subject to the control of the Court of Auditors". And that's the case! Here we are dealing with a matter subject to the control of the Court of Auditors. That is precisely why the Court of Auditors' audit possibilities are transferred to the Subcommittee, Mr Wurmitzer. ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e18444	Mr President. Mr. Minister! Mr President of the Court of Auditors! I look very briefly at the Court of Auditors' report on old-age rehabilitation. The Court of Auditors notes in its report that there is a very high lack of money, especially in the case of old-age rehabilitation. Over the last few years, the total revenue for the old load renovation has amounted to 1.2 billion schilling – but the need for the old load recovery would have been around 20 billion. These two figures clearly show how far needs and actual upswings divide. But the Court of Auditors not only criticises some of the things in its report, but also notes that many things have been made very positive, and I am now particularly referring to the fishing landfill. In the fishing landfill, groundwater protection measures were taken in the form of lock wells to secure the old load. These barrier wells, which have been built, have prevented further groundwater contamination in the area of fishing landfills and groundwater lakes, thus enabling better water quality to be supplied, and this in a very short time and with funds of about 22 million schilling annually. This was an emergency measure, because for technical reasons the disposal of the fishing landfill would not have taken place as quickly as possible. I should also like to point out in this connection that, although the Court of Auditors is criticising many areas, it has to be criticised, but at the same time it is also putting out very special positive things. I would like to thank the Court of Auditors once again for this. On the comments of Mr Meischberger: He said he would encourage the government parties to influence the design of the agenda. Mr Meischberger! The design of the agenda will be defined in the Presidency, and there your club is equally represented.

In conclusion: I would be pleased if, in other reports, such positive reports could be presented after the reviews. Thanks to the Court of Auditors for their conscientious work! Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1997-07-11-020-XX-NRSITZ-00083_d7e21443	Mr President. High house! As far as the Art Promotion Act is concerned, I would like to thank our colleagues Franz Morak for his tenacity. As one who comes from a rural region, I would like to note that the border regions or the federal states and the artists living there would have been severely disadvantaged if only the prices and scholarships awarded by the federal government had been exempt from the tax obligation. The departure of artists from rural areas and border regions would be of great disadvantage to these areas. I think it is extremely important that political measures do not lead to the ban on cultural life from certain places and only to large cities. Art should be accessible to all people in this country to the same extent. In my constituency in southern Styria, various initiatives have emerged in all areas of cultural life in recent years. Modern art policy must support and promote the dissemination of cultural life, as it is as important for tourism as for the cultural identity of the region. Fortunately, the People's Party, in cooperation with the coalition partner, has succeeded in a solution that prevents the creation of two classes of artists who would have had a financial advantage. I also welcome the amendment of this law because I consider prizes and scholarships to be a special consideration of outstanding achievements in the art sector. However, financial recognition must not lead to mere eye-catching. Prices and scholarships in the arts field are not so high that one can live or even become rich. Rather, they should give genuinely talented people an opportunity to realize their ideas and creativity. That is why I am glad that prices and scholarships awarded by the Federal Government or by the countries are not reduced by the Republic itself by the fact that they are subject to income tax. Finally, I would like to stress that, in the course of parliamentary treatment in the Committee on Culture, it has succeeded in eliminating bureaucratic inequality, which the people concerned would have rightly not understood, and in creating a clear and clear legal regime. I wish you all a quiet and hot summer with some relaxing cooling phase, in which a glass of good Austrian wine is very good. Thank you.
